I have definitely stepped back from quite a few things. \n\nI used to be fairly involved in mentoring, and I'd regularly go out to community events or music gatherings. Lately, I've just let those go. I haven't been making any travel plans, either. So while I'm still functioning fine day-to-day, my weeks are just... well, they're much smaller than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting as much pleasure out of the things you enjoy as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I'd say the amount of pleasure is about the same. Like I mentioned, when I sit down with a book or actually get together with friends, I still really enjoy it. It doesn't feel diminished or muted at all. It's just... well, I'm doing fewer things overall, but the things I do still feel just as good to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad, empty, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm in tears most days, or that it's anything dramatic. But... there is definitely this low, gray tone running underneath everything. It's just sort of there, even when I'm in the studio or sitting across from my wife at dinner. It's not a sharp sadness, just a constant, quiet emptiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this feeling of emptiness and the changes in your life are affecting your relationships with others, such as your wife?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. My wife and I have a pretty steady routine, and this hasn't caused any real friction between us. I mean, she obviously knows I'm staying in more these days, but we still talk and spend time together just fine. \n\nAs for other people... well, I'm seeing them less simply because I dropped out of those community events. But when I do see my friends, everything feels completely normal. So, no, I wouldn't say it's hurting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current feelings of emptiness and low mood as mildly bothersome or quite distressing?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's mildly bothersome. It's not something that's tearing me apart or making me feel distressed, really. It's just... well, it's just there. It's more of a nuisance, this constant low feeling, rather than something that's overwhelming me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray tone and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It's been quite a while now. It really started after a hiking injury I had... that was a little over three years ago. If I think about the exact timing, it's been about three years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. My appetite is about the same as it's always been, and I'm eating normally. As for sleep, I still get my usual seven or eight hours a night. So, no, nothing different in those areas.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this low, gray tone and emptiness you're feeling is something you can't stop thinking about, or is it more in the background of your daily thoughts?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more in the background. I mean, I don't sit around dwelling on it all day or anything like that. It's just... well, it's always kind of there while I'm going about my day. I certainly wouldn't say I can't stop thinking about it, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and this low, gray tone lifted?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I might have a decent afternoon here or there, but... no, I haven't had a stretch of two weeks where it completely went away. It's just been pretty steady since it started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this low, gray tone?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I feel low, sure, but I don't have any thoughts about dying or hurting myself. Nothing like that at all. It's just a... a persistent dullness, really. But no suicidal thoughts, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating or making decisions since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any issues with that. My focus seems to be fine. I can still sit down and read, or figure out what I need to get done around the house without any trouble. Making decisions hasn't been a problem either. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had any difficulty concentrating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I suppose in a quiet but stubborn sort of way, that the best version of my life is just behind me. Let's see... it's been about three years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before the injury?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes. I haven't really noticed a change there. I mean, I still enjoy reading a good book, and I like catching up with my friends when we get together. It's really just that underlying feeling I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this feeling of your life being behind you is something that mildly bothers you or significantly interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's somewhere in between. I still manage all the basics at home without any real trouble, so it hasn't completely stopped me in my tracks. But... I have definitely stepped back from quite a few things. \n\nI used to be fairly involved in mentoring, and I'd regularly go out to community events or music gatherings. Lately, I've just let those go. I haven't been making any travel plans, either. So while I'm still functioning fine day-to-day, my weeks are just... well, they're much smaller than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting as much pleasure out of the things you enjoy as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I'd say the amount of pleasure is about the same. Like I mentioned, when I sit down with a book or actually get together with friends, I still really enjoy it. It doesn't feel diminished or muted at all. It's just... well, I'm doing fewer things overall, but the things I do still feel just as good to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad, empty, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm in tears most days, or that it's anything dramatic. But... there is definitely this low, gray tone running underneath everything. It's just sort of there, even when I'm in the studio or sitting across from my wife at dinner. It's not a sharp sadness, just a constant, quiet emptiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this feeling of emptiness and the changes in your life are affecting your relationships with others, such as your wife?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. My wife and I have a pretty steady routine, and this hasn't caused any real friction between us. I mean, she obviously knows I'm staying in more these days, but we still talk and spend time together just fine. \n\nAs for other people... well, I'm seeing them less simply because I dropped out of those community events. So, no, I wouldn't say it's hurting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current feelings of emptiness and low mood as mildly bothersome or quite distressing?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's mildly bothersome. It's more of a nuisance, this constant low feeling, rather than something that's overwhelming me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray tone and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It's been quite a while now. It really started after a hiking injury I had... that was a little over three years ago. If I think about the exact timing, it's been about three years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. My appetite is about the same as it's always been, and I'm eating normally. As for sleep, I still get my usual seven or eight hours a night. So, no, nothing different in those areas.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this low, gray tone and emptiness you're feeling is something you can't stop thinking about, or is it more in the background of your daily thoughts?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more in the background. I mean, I don't sit around dwelling on it all day or anything like that. It's just... well, it's always kind of there while I'm going about my day. I certainly wouldn't say I can't stop thinking about it, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and this low, gray tone lifted?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I might have a decent afternoon here or there, but... no, I haven't had a stretch of two weeks where it completely went away. It's just been pretty steady since it started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this low, gray tone?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I feel low, sure, but I don't have any thoughts about dying or hurting myself. Nothing like that at all. It's just a... a persistent dullness, really. But no suicidal thoughts, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating or making decisions since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any issues with that. My focus seems to be fine. I can still sit down and read, or figure out what I need to get done around the house without any trouble. Making decisions hasn't been a problem either. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had any difficulty concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this low, gray tone and emptiness are affecting your interest in social activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still keep up with my friends and... well, I still go out and see family.
